## Checkpoint296 — Unbounded auxiliary mass and the polar metric

Example 15.6.8 on printed page 457 constructs a compact complex sixfold with a smooth strictly positive genuinely nonclosed background and unbounded bounded-potential mass. Two projective lines, the Hopf threefold and one further projective line form the product. A positive nonconstant coefficient h over the first two curves makes the vertical term nonclosed. Scaling the Hopf smooth potentials by c=min h preserves positivity; top-degree expansion gives the lower bound 120c^3 times their diverging threefold masses. The factor 120=6!/3! is explicit. The separated unit-divisor locus has a complete geodesic polar metric of diameter 4, closed ideal analytic closure and unique nearest-point 1-Lipschitz ideal retraction. Two analytic ideal models have polar distance 1, while every ordinary representative of each has zero extended mass. The product locus is explicit; the unrestricted topology problem is unchanged.

## Checkpoint294 — General auxiliary product models

Proposition 15.6.18 on page455 proves the following. Arbitrary compact complex auxiliary factors and smooth semipositive vertical terms preserve the separated curve envelope identities, complete geodesic polar metric, ideal analytic closure and nearest-point ideal retraction without an ambient mass bound. Zero-obstacle equality, atomic polar curvature, relative-envelope membership and equality at every smooth obstacle remain equivalent. All model comparison volumes vanish. Vanishing auxiliary terms or curve areas force nonbigness; positive curve areas and strictly positive vertical terms give bigness, including genuinely nonclosed examples. Genuine curve-product slices and countable-divisor domination compute the base projections. Unit-divisor reduction transfers them upstairs and gives zero mass for every ordinary representative. The common potential on Y times Z identifies the polar and actual analytic loci across auxiliary factors. Pullback of curve stretching competitors proves the obstacle criterion. Compact-fiber descent and closed top-degree auxiliary test forms prove the nonbig cases; smooth curve gauges prove bigness, and the mixed derivative component proves genuine nonclosedness.

## Checkpoint293 — All-obstacle separated product criterion

Lemma 15.6.6 on page452, Proposition 15.6.17 and Corollary 15.6.7 on page453 prove the following. Relative-envelope membership of a separated sum on a finite product is equivalent to membership of each coordinate in its own relative class. For arbitrary admissible curve backgrounds, zero-obstacle ordinary/ideal equality, equality of polar and atomic curvatures, relative membership in the ideal reference and equality at every smooth product obstacle are equivalent. Mixed obstacles, zero-area coordinates and zero-volume models are included. The same criterion holds through the nonnegative nonclosed auxiliary coefficient and unit-divisor extension. Add coordinate stretching competitors to obtain product membership; genuine coordinate restrictions and constant corrections give its converse. ## Checkpoint292 — Arbitrary curve product metric

Proposition 15.6.16 on page450 proves the following. The restricted unit-divisor product locus admits arbitrary smooth admissible curve backgrounds, including zero areas, with a complete geodesic sum polar metric, ideal analytic closure and unique nearest-point ideal retraction. Its diameter and bounded Hausdorff comparison are controlled exactly by curve areas in the ordered case. Area-decreasing maps are surjective ideal-commuting contractions with explicit lost-coordinate fiber diameters. Uniform zero-area degeneration converges in L1 to the singleton retaining the normalized smooth curve potentials. Separated curve projections and countable-divisor domination give the base model identities through every nonnegative auxiliary coefficient; unit-divisor reduction transfers them upstairs. Closed positive test forms prove nonbigness at zero auxiliary coefficient or any zero curve area. Curve polar classification and the weighted metric comparison transfer all metric statements. Actual rational atomic masses prove analytic density, while irrational area ratios need not preserve individual analytic models. Uniform curve zero-area collapse and fiber integration prove the product limit.

## Checkpoint290 — Weighted product metric comparison

## Checkpoint228 — All smooth obstacles

Sections 15.6.4 and 29.12.3 develop convergence for every smooth obstacle. The resulting uniformity is covariant under smooth gauges and positive scaling, and its analytic closure is exactly ordinary/ideal envelope equality for all smooth obstacles. The new pseudometric has complete canonical ideal loci, while the whole ordinary space remains incomplete even on a fixed positive-mass model family. On ordinary curve models its topology is strictly between the zero-obstacle topology and polar total variation.

## Checkpoint226 — Ordinary singularity-type topology

## Checkpoint202 — Polar curvature and all curve models

Section 29.12 is rewritten as Model potentials on curves. Every normalized ordinary model is uniquely determined by its polar curvature measure, including zero mass and diffuse polar data. For arbitrary curve potentials the upper-volume comparison equals total variation of their polar measures. The model space is complete and geodesic. Its ideal models form the closed complete analytic closure; the ideal envelope is a 1-Lipschitz retraction selecting the unique nearest ideal model, with distance equal to the nonatomic polar mass. Chapter 1 supplies direct proofs of the planar inverse maximum principle and the polar order/maximum identities. ## Checkpoint201 — The zero-volume boundary on curves

New Section 29.12 treats the zero-volume boundary on curves. On all ideal models, the upper-volume comparison is exactly total variation of atomic curvature, and this complete space is the completion of positive-mass analytic ordinary models. For every curve potential, its infimum comparison to these analytic models is exactly its nonatomic polar curvature mass; vanishing is equivalent to equality of ordinary and ideal envelopes. A diffuse Cantor measure on a polar set yields a zero-mass ordinary model with trivial multiplier ideals and approximation defect A. ## Checkpoint200 — A complete metric on positive-volume models

New Section 29.11 gives a triangle-inequality counterexample for the upper-volume formula using intersecting divisors on the projective plane. It proves lower-volume continuity and constructs a complete compatible metric on all positive-lower-volume ordinary models. ## Continuing work

Continue focused correctness and readability reviews under the new chapter structure, preserving the full scope of the source book. The moved statements have no stronger conclusions. The main/conditional distinction should remain visible in every future addition; a hypothesis on one datum or reference is not a uniform mass property.

Before using any result of Popovici, consult the supplied private working reference, then independently check the complete proof and dependencies. Do not integrate that reference into the book or package. The historical uses and exact proof checks are in `research/editorial/popovici-use-review.md`; the former Nakamaye use is now inactive.

The individual-density subsolution threshold, Gauduchon duality and exponential approximations remain central in Chapters13 and26. Positive threshold alone is not known here to give a general exact equation when lower mass vanishes. Keep the additional moment, envelope and smooth-dual assumptions explicit. Chapter14's functional convergence does not imply recovery of the full singularity type.

Chapter19's curve source metric compares with total variation of atomic data under positive mass. This remains distinct from the elementary ideal metric and its Chapter15 mass discontinuity. General Hermitian ordinary-type topology, higher-dimensional all-model trace realization and lower partial Bergman asymptotics remain open. Chapter33's primitive elliptic bundles and K3 test retain their checked metric and restricted-mass calculations; unrestricted lower mass in the K3 example remains unresolved. The historical current-descent suggestion is unproved and outside the book.

Checkpoint90 was remotely verified at `0f3dfa696032aa543352acb019836de7547e4da5`. Review the full eligible diff and run the authorized private synchronization command after the release checks. Git history and an exact remote-head check record backup; do not infer successful publication merely from a local commit. Keep the original template, upright differential d, standard environments, standard ideal-envelope notation and no formal NUM citation. Never edit verifier records.
